					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">Irish abbess and saint</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">

</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1066479718">.mw-parser-output href="#cite_note-irishcatholic.ie-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Gaelic_Ireland" title="Gaelic Ireland">Gaelic Ireland</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data"><span title="circa">c.</span><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;525</span> (age 74)<br /><a href="/wiki/Kildare" title="Kildare">Kildare</a>, <a href="/wiki/Leinster" Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">Feast</a></th><td class="infobox-data">1 February</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Saint_symbolism" title="Saint symbolism">Attributes</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Brigid%27s_cross" title="Brigid&#39;s cross">Brigid's cross</a>; crozier of an abbess; flames or lamp; cow or geese<sup id="cite_ref-Farmer_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Farmer-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">Patronage</a></th><td class="infobox-data">Kildare; Ireland; healers; poets; blacksmiths; livestock and dairy workers<sup id="cite_ref-Farmer_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Farmer-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Saint Brigid of Kildare</b> or <b>Brigid of Ireland</b> (<a href="/wiki/Irish_language" title="Irish language">Irish</a>: <i lang="ga">Naomh Bríd</i>; <a href="/wiki/Classical_Gaelic" title="Classical Gaelic">Classical Gaelic</a>: <i>Brighid</i>; <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Brigida</i>; <span title="circa">c.</span><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;451</span>&#160;&#8211;&#32;525) is the <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patroness saint</a> (or 'mother saint') of Ireland, and one of its three national saints along with <a href="/wiki/Saint_Patrick" title="Saint Patrick">Patrick</a> and <a href="/wiki/Columba" title="Columba">Columba</a>. According to medieval Irish <a href="/wiki/Hagiography" title="Hagiography">hagiographies</a>, she was an <a href="/wiki/Abbess" title="Abbess">abbess</a> who founded the important abbey of <a href="/wiki/Kildare_Abbey" title="Kildare Abbey">Kildare</a> (<i>Cill Dara</i>),<sup id="cite_ref-Jestice2004_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jestice2004-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> as well as several other <a href="/wiki/Convent" title="Convent">convents</a> of nuns. There are few historical facts about her, and her hagiographies are mainly anecdotes and miracle tales, some of which are rooted in <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Celtic_religion" title="Ancient Celtic religion">pagan</a> folklore.<sup id="cite_ref-Farmer_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Farmer-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> They say Brigid was the daughter of a chieftain and a slave woman, and was raised in a <a href="/wiki/Druid" title="Druid">druid</a>'s household before becoming a <a href="/wiki/Consecrated_virgin" title="Consecrated virgin">consecrated virgin</a>. She is patroness of many things, including poetry, learning, healing, protection, blacksmithing, livestock and dairy production. In her honour, a <a href="/wiki/Perpetual_fire" class="mw-redirect" title="Perpetual fire">perpetual fire</a> was kept burning at Kildare for centuries.
</p><p>Some historians suggest that Brigid is a Christianization of the Celtic goddess, <a href="/wiki/Brigid" title="Brigid">Bríd</a>. St Brigid's <a href="/wiki/St_Brigid%27s_Day" class="mw-redirect" title="St Brigid&#39;s Day">feast day</a> is 1 February, and traditionally it involves weaving <a href="/wiki/Brigid%27s_cross" title="Brigid&#39;s cross">Brigid's crosses</a> and many other folk customs. It was originally a pre-Christian festival called <a href="/wiki/Imbolc" title="Imbolc">Imbolc</a>, marking the beginning of spring. From 2023 it is a <a href="/wiki/Public_holiday_in_the_Republic_of_Ireland" class="mw-redirect" title="Public holiday in the Republic of Ireland">public holiday in the Republic of Ireland</a>, the first named after a woman.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> This feast day is shared by <a href="/wiki/Dar_Lugdach" title="Dar Lugdach">Dar Lugdach</a>, who tradition says was her student, close companion, and successor. The occasion is marked on the first Monday on or after 1st February.
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r886046785">.mw-parser-output .toclimit-2 .toclevel-1 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-3 .toclevel-2 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-4 .toclevel-3 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-5 .toclevel-4 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-6 .toclevel-5 ul,.mw-parser-output .toclimit-7 .toclevel-6 ul{display:none}</style><div class="toclimit-3"><meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" /></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Name">Name</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Name">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The saint has the same name as the goddess <a href="/wiki/Brigid" title="Brigid">Brigid</a>, derived from the <a href="/wiki/Proto-Celtic" class="mw-redirect" title="Proto-Celtic">Proto-Celtic</a> <span title="Proto-Celtic-language text">&#42;<i lang="cel">Brigantī</i></span> "high, exalted" and ultimately originating with <a href="/wiki/Proto-Indo-European" class="mw-redirect" title="Proto-Indo-European">Proto-Indo-European</a> <span title="Proto-Indo-European-language text">&#42;<i lang="ine">bʰerǵʰ-</i></span>. In <a href="/wiki/Old_Irish" title="Old Irish">Old Irish</a> her name was spelled <span title="Old Irish (to 900)-language text"><i lang="sga">Brigit</i></span><sup id="cite_ref-:0_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> and pronounced <small></small><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="sga-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA" title="Help:IPA">[ˈbʲrʲiɣʲidʲ]</a></span>. In <a href="/wiki/Modern_Irish" class="mw-redirect" title="Modern Irish">Modern Irish</a> she is also called <span title="Irish-language text"><i lang="ga">Bríd</i></span>.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> In <a href="/wiki/Welsh_language" title="Welsh language">Welsh</a> she is called <span title="Welsh-language text"><i lang="cy">Ffraid</i></span> (sometimes <a href="/wiki/Lenition" title="Lenition">mutated</a> to <span title="Welsh-language text"><i lang="cy">Fraid</i></span>), such as in several places called <a href="/wiki/Llansanffraid_(disambiguation)" class="mw-redirect mw-disambig" title="Llansanffraid (disambiguation)">Llansanffraid</a>, "St. Brigit's church". She is also referred to as "the Mary of the <a href="/wiki/Gaels" title="Gaels">Gael</a>"<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> and the "Mother Saint of Ireland".<sup id="cite_ref-stations_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-stations-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Historicity">Historicity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Historicity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>There is debate over whether Brigid was a real person. There are few historical facts about her, and early <a href="/wiki/Hagiography" title="Hagiography">hagiographies</a> "are mainly anecdotes and miracle stories, some of which are deeply rooted in Irish pagan folklore".<sup id="cite_ref-Farmer_3-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Farmer-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> She has the same name and many of the same attributes as the Celtic goddess <a href="/wiki/Brigid" title="Brigid">Brigid</a>, and there are many supernatural events and folk customs associated with her.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Like the saint, the goddess in Irish myth is associated with poetry, healing, protection, smithcraft, and domestic animals, according to <i><a href="/wiki/Sanas_Cormaic" title="Sanas Cormaic">Sanas Cormaic</a></i> and <i><a href="/wiki/Lebor_Gab%C3%A1la_%C3%89renn" title="Lebor Gabála Érenn">Lebor Gabála Érenn</a></i>. Furthermore, the saint's feast day falls on the Gaelic traditional festival of <a href="/wiki/Imbolc" title="Imbolc">Imbolc</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ohogain_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ohogain-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 60–61">&#58;&#8202;60–61&#8202;</span></sup> Some scholars suggest that the saint is a <a href="/wiki/Christianization_of_saints_and_feasts" title="Christianization of saints and feasts">Christianisation</a> of the goddess; others that she was a real person whose mythos took on the goddess's attributes. Medieval art historian Pamela Berger argues that Christian monks "took the ancient figure of the <a href="/wiki/Mother_goddess" title="Mother goddess">mother goddess</a> and grafted her name and functions onto her Christian counterpart".<sup id="cite_ref-Berger_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Berger-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/D%C3%A1ith%C3%AD_%C3%93_h%C3%93g%C3%A1in" title="Dáithí Ó hÓgáin">Dáithí Ó hÓgáin</a> and others suggest that the saint had been chief <a href="/wiki/Druid" title="Druid">druid</a> at the temple of the goddess Brigid, was responsible for converting it into a Christian monastery, and that after her death, the name and characteristics of the goddess became attached to the saint.<sup id="cite_ref-ohogain_10-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ohogain-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-wb_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wb-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Noel Kissane, in a piece in <i><a href="/wiki/The_Catholic_Historical_Review" title="The Catholic Historical Review">The Catholic Historical Review</a></i>, argues she was a historical figure.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life">Life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Kildare_-_Kildare_Cathedral_-_20220922115505.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Kildare_-_Kildare_Cathedral_-_20220922115505.jpg/220px-Kildare_-_Kildare_Cathedral_-_20220922115505.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Kildare_-_Kildare_Cathedral_-_20220922115505.jpg/330px-Kildare_-_Kildare_Cathedral_-_20220922115505.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/Kildare_-_Kildare_Cathedral_-_20220922115505.jpg/440px-Kildare_-_Kildare_Cathedral_-_20220922115505.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1427" data-file-height="1070" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Kildare_-_Kildare_Cathedral_-_20220922115505.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Kildare_Cathedral" title="Kildare Cathedral">Kildare Cathedral</a>, built on the site of the original abbey said to have been founded by Brigid</div></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sources">Sources</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Sources">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Among the most ancient accounts of St Brigid are two <a href="/wiki/Old_Irish" title="Old Irish">Old Irish</a> hymns; the first by St <a href="/wiki/Ultan_of_Ardbraccan" title="Ultan of Ardbraccan">Ultan of Ardbraccan</a> (died c. 657), <i>Brigit Bé Bithmaith</i> ('Brigid ever-excellent woman') also known as "Ultan's hymn",<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> and the second is "Broccán's hymn", composed by St <a href="/wiki/Brocc%C3%A1n_Cl%C3%B3en" title="Broccán Clóen">Broccán Clóen</a> (died c. 650) at the request of Ultan who was his tutor.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Two early Lives of St Brigid in <a href="/wiki/Hiberno-Latin" title="Hiberno-Latin">Hiberno-Latin</a> prose, the <i>Vita Sanctae Brigitae</i> I and II, were written in the 7th–8th centuries, the first one possibly by <a href="/wiki/Ailer%C3%A1n" title="Ailerán">St Aleran</a> (died in 665), lector of Clonard, the second by <a href="/wiki/Cogitosus" title="Cogitosus">Cogitosus</a>, a monk of Kildare.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> An Old Irish prose Life, <i>Bethu Brigte</i>, was composed in the 9th century.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Several later Latin and Irish Lives of the saint were composed. The <i>Vita</i> III, in hexameter verse, is sometimes attributed to St Coelan of <a href="/wiki/Inishcaltra" title="Inishcaltra">Inishcaltra</a> of the 7th–8th centuries,<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> but appears more likely to have been written by St <a href="/wiki/Donatus_of_Fiesole" title="Donatus of Fiesole">Donatus</a>, an Irish monk who became Bishop of <a href="/wiki/Fiesole_Cathedral" title="Fiesole Cathedral">Fiesole</a> in 824.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> In Donatus' prolog, it refers to the earlier Lives by St Ultan (see before for his hymn), St. Aleran (see "Vita I") and an Anonymus.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> A 34-<a href="/wiki/Hexameter" title="Hexameter">hexameter</a> Latin poem about St Brigid had previously been composed by the Irish Roman cleric <a href="/wiki/Colman_nepos_Cracavist" title="Colman nepos Cracavist">Colman</a> c. 800.
</p><p>Discussion on dates for the annals and the accuracy of dates relating to St Brigid continues.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Because of the legendary quality of the earliest accounts of her life, there is debate among many secular scholars and Christians as to the truthfulness of her biographies. According to tradition, Brigid was born in the year 451 in <a href="/wiki/Faughart" title="Faughart">Faughart</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Joyce_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Joyce-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> just north of <a href="/wiki/Dundalk" title="Dundalk">Dundalk</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Story_of_St._Brigid_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Story_of_St._Brigid-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-irishcatholic.ie_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-irishcatholic.ie-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> in <a href="/wiki/Conaille_Muirtheimne" title="Conaille Muirtheimne">Conaille Muirtheimne</a>, part of the <a href="/wiki/Ulaid" title="Ulaid">Kingdom of Ulaid</a>. All early sources say she was one of the <a href="/wiki/Fothairt" title="Fothairt">Fothairt</a>, a people mainly based in <a href="/wiki/Leinster" title="Leinster">Leinster</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ohogain_10-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ohogain-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Three biographies name her mother as Broicsech, a slave who had been baptised by <a href="/wiki/Saint_Patrick" title="Saint Patrick">Saint Patrick</a>. They name her father as Dubhthach, a chieftain of Leinster.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <i>Vitae</i> says that Dubhthach's wife forced him to sell Brigid's mother to a <a href="/wiki/Druid" title="Druid">druid</a> when she became pregnant.<sup id="cite_ref-ohogain_10-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ohogain-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> This might have been inspired by the Biblical story of <a href="/wiki/Abraham" title="Abraham">Abraham</a> and <a href="/wiki/Hagar" title="Hagar">Hagar</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Charles-Edwards_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Charles-Edwards-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> An 8th century account calls the druid Maithghean.<sup id="cite_ref-ohogain_10-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ohogain-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> It says Broicsech gave birth to Brigid at dawn, on the threshold, while bringing milk into the druid's house. This <a href="/wiki/Liminality" title="Liminality">liminality</a> seems to be a vestige of druidic lore.<sup id="cite_ref-ohogain_10-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ohogain-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Brigid was thus born into slavery. Legends of her early holiness include her vomiting when the druid tried to feed her, due to his impurity; a white cow with red ears arrives to sustain her instead.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Brigid's druid stepfather is portrayed somewhat sympathetically in the stories. He can see that Brigid is special, he is concerned for Brigid's welfare, and he eventually frees her and her mother.<sup id="cite_ref-Charles-Edwards_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Charles-Edwards-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Cogitosus said she spent her youth as a farm worker; churning butter, shepherding the flocks and tending the harvest.<sup id="cite_ref-ohogain_10-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ohogain-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As she grew older, Brigid was said to have worked miracles, including healing and feeding the poor. According to one tale, as a child, she once gave away her mother's entire store of butter. The butter was then replenished in answer to Brigid's prayers.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 13">&#58;&#8202;13&#8202;</span></sup> Around the age of ten, she was returned as a household servant to her father, where her charity led her to donate his belongings to anyone who asked.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In both of the earliest biographies, Dubhthach is so annoyed with Brigid that he took her in a chariot to <a href="/wiki/Crimthann_mac_%C3%89nnai" title="Crimthann mac Énnai">the King of Leinster</a> to sell her. While Dubhthach was talking to the king, Brigid gave away her father's bejewelled sword to a beggar to barter it for food to feed his family. The king recognised her holiness and convinced Dubhthach to grant his daughter freedom.<sup id="cite_ref-Bitel_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bitel-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religious_life">Religious life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Religious life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Brigid_by_Patrick_Joseph_Tuohy.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/Saint_Brigid_by_Patrick_Joseph_Tuohy.jpg/220px-Saint_Brigid_by_Patrick_Joseph_Tuohy.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="291" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/Saint_Brigid_by_Patrick_Joseph_Tuohy.jpg/330px-Saint_Brigid_by_Patrick_Joseph_Tuohy.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4b/Saint_Brigid_by_Patrick_Joseph_Tuohy.jpg/440px-Saint_Brigid_by_Patrick_Joseph_Tuohy.jpg 2x" data-file-width="605" data-file-height="800" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Brigid_by_Patrick_Joseph_Tuohy.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Saint Brigid by Patrick Joseph Tuohy</div></div></div>
<p>It is said that Brigid was "veiled" or became a <a href="/wiki/Consecrated_virgin" title="Consecrated virgin">consecrated virgin</a> either through Saint Mac Caille, Bishop of <a href="/wiki/Croghan_Hill" title="Croghan Hill">Cruachán Brí Éile</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> or by St <a href="/wiki/M%C3%A9l_of_Ardagh" title="Mél of Ardagh">Mél of Ardagh</a> at Mág Tulach (the present barony of <a href="/wiki/Tyrrellspass" title="Tyrrellspass">Fartullagh, County Westmeath</a>), who gave her the powers of an <a href="/wiki/Abbess" title="Abbess">abbess</a>.
</p><p>According to tradition, around 480 Brigid founded a monastery at <a href="/wiki/Kildare" title="Kildare">Kildare</a> (<i>Cill Dara</i>, "church of the oak"), on the site of a pagan shrine to the Celtic goddess Brigid, served by a group of young women who tended an <a href="/wiki/Eternal_flame" title="Eternal flame">eternal flame</a>. The site was under a large oak tree on the ridge of Druim Criadh.<sup id="cite_ref-Kildare_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Kildare-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Brigid, with an initial group of seven companions, is credited with organising communal consecrated religious life for women in Ireland.<sup id="cite_ref-cna_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cna-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> She founded two monasteries; one for men, the other for women. Brigid became the first Abbess of Kildare and invited <a href="/wiki/Conleth" title="Conleth">Conleth</a> (<i>Conláed</i>), a <a href="/wiki/Hermit" title="Hermit">hermit</a> from <a href="/wiki/Connell_(barony)" title="Connell (barony)">Connell</a>, to help her; he became the first <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Kildare" title="Bishop of Kildare">Bishop of Kildare</a>. It has often been said that she gave canonical jurisdiction to Conleth, but Archbishop Healy says that she simply "selected the person to whom the Church gave this jurisdiction", and her biographer tells us that she chose Saint Conleth "to govern the church along with herself". For centuries, Kildare was ruled by a double line of abbot-bishops and of abbesses, the Abbess of Kildare being regarded as <a href="/wiki/Superior_general" class="mw-redirect" title="Superior general">superior general</a> of the monasteries in Ireland.<sup id="cite_ref-catholic_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catholic-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Her successors have always been accorded episcopal honour.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Brigid's oratory at Kildare became a centre of religion and learning, and developed into a cathedral city.<sup id="cite_ref-catholic_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catholic-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Brigid is credited with founding a school of art, including metalwork and illumination, which Conleth oversaw. The Kildare <a href="/wiki/Scriptorium" title="Scriptorium">scriptorium</a> made the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Kildare" class="mw-redirect" title="Book of Kildare">Book of Kildare</a>, which drew high praise from <a href="/wiki/Gerald_of_Wales" title="Gerald of Wales">Gerald of Wales</a> (Giraldus Cambrensis), but disappeared during the <a href="/wiki/Protestant_Reformation" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant Reformation">Reformation</a>. According to Giraldus, nothing that he ever saw was at all comparable to the book, every page of which was gorgeously illuminated, and the interlaced work and the harmony of the colours left the impression that "all this is the work of angelic, and not human skill".<sup id="cite_ref-catholic_32-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catholic-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to the <i><a href="/wiki/Trias_Thaumaturga" class="mw-redirect" title="Trias Thaumaturga">Trias Thaumaturga</a></i> Brigid spent time in <a href="/wiki/Connacht" title="Connacht">Connacht</a> and founded many churches in the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Elphin" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Elphin">Diocese of Elphin</a>. She is said to have visited Longford, Tipperary, Limerick, and South Leinster.<sup id="cite_ref-Joyce_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Joyce-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> Her friendship with Saint Patrick is noted in the following paragraph from the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Armagh" title="Book of Armagh">Book of Armagh</a>: <i>"inter sanctum Patricium Brigitanque Hibernesium columpnas amicitia caritatis inerat tanta, ut unum cor consiliumque haberent unum. Christus per illum illamque virtutes multas peregit"</i> ("Between St. Patrick and St. Brigid, the pillars of the Irish people, there was so great a friendship of charity that they had but one heart and one mind. Through him and through her Christ performed many great works".)<sup id="cite_ref-catholic_32-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catholic-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> However, given that the 'historical' Brigid was born in 451 and Saint Patrick died circa 461, this is unlikely.
</p><p>The monk <a href="/wiki/Ultan_of_Ardbraccan" title="Ultan of Ardbraccan">Ultan of Ardbraccan</a>, who wrote a life of Brigid, recounts a story that <a href="/wiki/Dar_Lugdach" title="Dar Lugdach">Darlugdach</a>, Brigid's favourite pupil, fell in love with a young man and, hoping to meet him, sneaked out of the bed in which she and Brigid were sleeping. However, recognising her spiritual peril, she prayed for guidance, then placed burning embers in her shoes and put them on. "Thus, by fire", Ultan wrote, "she put out fire, and by pain extinguished pain."<sup id="cite_ref-TO_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TO-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> She then returned to bed. Brigid feigned sleep but was aware of Darlugdach's departure. The next day, Darlugdach revealed to Brigid the experience of the night before. Brigid reassured her that she was "now safe from the fire of passion and the fire of hell hereafter"  and then healed her student's feet.<sup id="cite_ref-TO_34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TO-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The name Darlugdach (also spelled Dar Lugdach or Dar Lughdacha) means "daughter of the god <a href="/wiki/Lugh" title="Lugh">Lugh</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-wb_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-wb-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 41">&#58;&#8202;41&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Duncan_(1913)_Saint_Bride.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/John_Duncan_%281913%29_Saint_Bride.jpg/220px-John_Duncan_%281913%29_Saint_Bride.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="187" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/John_Duncan_%281913%29_Saint_Bride.jpg/330px-John_Duncan_%281913%29_Saint_Bride.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ee/John_Duncan_%281913%29_Saint_Bride.jpg/440px-John_Duncan_%281913%29_Saint_Bride.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1920" data-file-height="1634" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Duncan_(1913)_Saint_Bride.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>St Brigid being carried away by angels, in a painting by <a href="/wiki/John_Duncan_(painter)" title="John Duncan (painter)">John Duncan</a> (1913)</div></div></div>
<p>Brigid is said to have been given the <a href="/wiki/Last_rites" title="Last rites">last rites</a> when she was dying by Saint Ninnidh of the Pure Hand. Afterward, he reportedly had his right hand encased in metal so that it would never be defiled, and this was the origin of his epithet.<sup id="cite_ref-catholic_32-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catholic-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Tradition says she died at Kildare on 1 February 525.<sup id="cite_ref-sisters_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-sisters-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Upon Brigid's death, Darlugdach became the second abbess of Kildare. Darlugdach was so devoted to her mentor that when Brigid lay dying Darlugdach expressed the wish to die with her, but Brigid replied that Darlugdach would die on the anniversary of her (Brigid's) death.<sup id="cite_ref-TO_34-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TO-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholicism" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholicism">Catholic Church</a> records Brigid's year of death as 521 and Darlugdach's as 522 and has assigned 1 February as the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">feast day</a> of both saints.<sup id="cite_ref-TO_34-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TO-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Miracles">Miracles</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Miracles">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Lorenzo_Lotto_-_Blessings_of_St_Bridget_(detail)_-_WGA13688.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Lorenzo_Lotto_-_Blessings_of_St_Bridget_%28detail%29_-_WGA13688.jpg/220px-Lorenzo_Lotto_-_Blessings_of_St_Bridget_%28detail%29_-_WGA13688.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="256" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Lorenzo_Lotto_-_Blessings_of_St_Bridget_%28detail%29_-_WGA13688.jpg/330px-Lorenzo_Lotto_-_Blessings_of_St_Bridget_%28detail%29_-_WGA13688.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/20/Lorenzo_Lotto_-_Blessings_of_St_Bridget_%28detail%29_-_WGA13688.jpg/440px-Lorenzo_Lotto_-_Blessings_of_St_Bridget_%28detail%29_-_WGA13688.jpg 2x" data-file-width="722" data-file-height="839" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Lorenzo_Lotto_-_Blessings_of_St_Bridget_(detail)_-_WGA13688.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A 16th-century <a href="/wiki/Fresco" title="Fresco">fresco</a> by <a href="/wiki/Lorenzo_Lotto" title="Lorenzo Lotto">Lorenzo Lotto</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Suardi_Chapel" title="Suardi Chapel">Suardi Chapel</a> showing Brigid turning water into beer (left) and healing a blind man (right)</div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Thomas_Charles-Edwards" title="Thomas Charles-Edwards">Thomas Charles-Edwards</a> wrote that Brigid's power is expressed in 'helping' miracles: healing, feeding the hungry, and rescuing the weak from violence. Unlike Saint Patrick, "most of her miracles were humble affairs for people of low rank" and she "never dictates the course of dynastic politics".<sup id="cite_ref-Charles-Edwards_26-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Charles-Edwards-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/D%C3%A1ith%C3%AD_%C3%93_h%C3%93g%C3%A1in" title="Dáithí Ó hÓgáin">Dáithí Ó hÓgáin</a> wrote that the melding of pagan goddess and Christian saint can be seen in some of the miracles, where Brigid multiplies food, bestows cattle and sheep, controls the weather, and is associated with fire or thermal springs.<sup id="cite_ref-ohogain_10-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ohogain-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to Brian Wright, the miracles of Brigid outlined by Cogitosus mostly concern healing; charity; cows, sheep and dairy; the harvest; fire; fertility/pregnancy; and her virginity/holiness.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li>One of the most well-known stories is of Brigid asking the King of Leinster for land. She told the king that the place where she stood was the perfect spot for a convent. It was beside a forest where they could gather firewood and berries, there was a lake nearby that would provide water, and the land was fertile. The king laughed at her and refused to give her any land. Brigid prayed and asked God to soften the king's heart. Then she smiled at the king and said, "Will you give me as much land as my cloak will cover?" The king thought that she was joking and agreed. She told four of her sisters to take up the cloak, but instead of laying it flat on the turf, each sister, with face turned to a different point of the compass, began to run swiftly. The cloak grew in all directions and covered many acres of land. "Oh, Brigid!" said the frightened king, "what are you about?". "I am, or rather my cloak is about covering your whole province to punish you for your stinginess to the poor". "Call your maidens back. I will give you a decent plot of ground." The saint was persuaded, and if the king held his purse-strings tight in the future, she had only to allude to her cloak to bring him to reason. Soon afterward, the king became a Christian, began to help the poor, and commissioned the building of the convent.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> This story was probably inspired by that of <a href="/wiki/Dido" title="Dido">Dido</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ohogain_10-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ohogain-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>After Brigid promised God a life of chastity, her brothers were annoyed at the loss of a <a href="/wiki/Bride_price" title="Bride price">bride price</a>. When she was outside carrying a load past a group of poor people, some began to laugh at her. A man named Bacene said to her, "The beautiful eye which is in your head will be betrothed to a man though you like it or not". In response, Brigid thrust her finger in her eye and said, "Here is that beautiful eye for you. I deem it unlikely that anyone will ask you for a blind girl". Her brothers tried to save her and wash away the blood from her wound, but there was no water to be found. Brigid said to them, "Put my staff about this sod in front of you", and after they did, a stream came forth from the ground. Then she said to Bacene, "Soon your two eyes will burst in your head", and it happened as she said.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_25-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> This story was probably inspired by the lore of <a href="/wiki/Saint_Lucy" title="Saint Lucy">Saint Lucy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ohogain_10-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ohogain-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>When Brigid was of marital age, a man by the name of <a href="/wiki/Dubthach_maccu_Lugair" title="Dubthach maccu Lugair">Dubthach maccu Lugair</a> came to woo her. Since Brigid had offered her virginity to God, she told the man that she could not accept him but that he should go to the woods behind his house where he would find a beautiful maiden to marry. Everything that he said to the maiden's parents would be pleasing to them. The man followed her instructions and Brigid's prophecy came true.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_25-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Brigid, who was an expert dairywoman and brewer, was reputed to turn water into beer.<sup id="cite_ref-Bitel_28-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bitel-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The prayers of Saint Brigid were said to still the wind and the rain.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In one story, Brigid protected a woman from a nobleman who had entrusted a silver brooch to the woman for safekeeping but then secretly had thrown it into the sea. He charged her with stealing it, knowing that he could take her as a slave if a <a href="/wiki/Brehon" title="Brehon">judge</a> ruled in his favour. The woman fled and sought refuge with Brigid's community. By chance, one of her fishermen caught a fish which, when cut open, was found to have swallowed the brooch. The nobleman freed the woman, confessed his sin, and bowed in submission to Brigid.<sup id="cite_ref-Bitel_28-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bitel-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> This is also an Irish version of a widespread story; another version is told of <a href="/wiki/Saint_Mungo" title="Saint Mungo">Saint Mungo</a>/Kentigern.<sup id="cite_ref-ohogain_10-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ohogain-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>When Brigid was travelling to see a doctor for a headache, she stayed at the house of a Leinster couple who had two mute daughters. The daughters were travelling with Brigid when her horse startled, causing her to fall and graze her head on a stone. A touch of Brigid's blood healed the girls of their muteness.<sup id="cite_ref-Bitel_28-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bitel-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Brigid is said to have preserved a nun's chastity in unusual circumstances. Liam de Paor (1993)<sup id="cite_ref-ldp_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ldp-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> and Connolly &amp; Picard (1987), in their complete translations of Cogitosus, give substantially the same translation<sup id="cite_ref-ldp_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ldp-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 211">&#58;&#8202;211&#8202;</span></sup> of the account of Brigid's ministry to a nun who had failed to keep her vow of chastity and had become pregnant. In the 1987 translation: "A certain woman who had taken the vow of chastity fell, through the youthful desire of pleasure, and her womb swelled with child. Brigid, exercising the most potent strength of her ineffable faith, blessed her, causing the child to disappear, without coming to birth, and without pain. She faithfully returned the woman to health and to penance". The Brigid Alliance, an American NGO that assists people seeking abortions, was named after St Brigid in reference to this miracle.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>When on the bank of the <a href="/wiki/River_Inny,_Westmeath" class="mw-redirect" title="River Inny, Westmeath">River Inny</a>, Brigid was given a gift of apples and sweet sloes. She later entered a house where many lepers begged her for these apples, which she offered willingly. The woman who had given the gift to Brigid was angered by this, saying that she had not given the gift to the lepers. Brigid was angry at the woman for withholding from the lepers and cursed her trees so they would no longer bear fruit.  Another woman gave Brigid the same gift, and again Brigid gave them to begging lepers. This woman asked that she and her garden be blessed. Brigid said that a large tree in the garden would have twofold fruit from its offshoots, and this came true.<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_25-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Brigid%27s_cross.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bd/Saint_Brigid%27s_cross.jpg/220px-Saint_Brigid%27s_cross.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="214" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bd/Saint_Brigid%27s_cross.jpg/330px-Saint_Brigid%27s_cross.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bd/Saint_Brigid%27s_cross.jpg/440px-Saint_Brigid%27s_cross.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1566" data-file-height="1520" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Brigid%27s_cross.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Brigid%27s_cross" title="Brigid&#39;s cross">Saint Brigid's Cross</a> or <i>Crosóg Bhríde</i></div></div></div>
<p>Brigid is honored on 1 February in the calendars of the <a href="/wiki/National_calendars_of_the_Roman_Rite#Ireland" title="National calendars of the Roman Rite">Catholic Church in Ireland</a>, as well as the <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican</a> <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Ireland" title="Church of Ireland">Church of Ireland</a>, <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">Church of England</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>She is a patroness saint of Ireland (and one of its three national saints), as well as of healers, poets, blacksmiths, livestock and dairy workers, among others.<sup id="cite_ref-Farmer_3-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Farmer-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Brigid is said to have been buried at the right of the high altar of <a href="/wiki/Kildare_Cathedral" title="Kildare Cathedral">Kildare Cathedral</a>, and a costly tomb raised over her<sup id="cite_ref-catholic_32-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catholic-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> "adorned with gems and precious stones and crowns of gold and silver".<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the late 12th century, <a href="/wiki/Gerald_of_Wales" title="Gerald of Wales">Gerald of Wales</a> wrote that nineteen nuns took turns keeping a <a href="/wiki/Perpetual_fire" class="mw-redirect" title="Perpetual fire">perpetual fire</a> burning at Kildare in honour of Brigid. It was said this fire was kept burning since Brigid's time, and it is suggested it was originally part of a temple of Brigit the goddess.<sup id="cite_ref-Butler&#39;s_Lives_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Butler&#39;s_Lives-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Gerald said it was ringed by a hedge that no man was allowed to cross.<sup id="cite_ref-Farmer_3-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Farmer-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="St_Brigid.27s_Day"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="St_Brigid's_Day">St Brigid's Day</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: St Brigid&#039;s Day">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Saint_Brigid%27s_Day" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Brigid&#39;s Day">Saint Brigid's Day</a></div>
<p>Saint Brigid's feast day is 1 February. Cogitosus, writing in the late 7th century, is the first to mention a feast day of Saint Brigid being observed in Kildare on this date.<sup id="cite_ref-ohogain_10-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ohogain-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> It was also the date of <a href="/wiki/Imbolc" title="Imbolc">Imbolc</a>, a seasonal festival that is believed to have pre-Christian origins.<sup id="cite_ref-ohogain_10-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ohogain-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-stations_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-stations-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Imbolc is one of four Gaelic seasonal festivals, along with <a href="/wiki/Bealtaine" class="mw-redirect" title="Bealtaine">Bealtaine</a> (1 May), <a href="/wiki/Lughnasa" class="mw-redirect" title="Lughnasa">Lughnasa</a> (1 August), and <a href="/wiki/Samhain" title="Samhain">Samhain</a> (1 November).<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The customs of Saint Brigid's Day did not begin to be recorded in detail until the early modern era. <a href="/wiki/Brigid%27s_cross" title="Brigid&#39;s cross">Brigid's crosses</a> are traditionally made on her feast day. These are three- or four-armed crosses woven from <a href="/wiki/Juncus" title="Juncus">rushes</a>. They are hung over doors and windows for protection against fire, lightning, illness and evil spirits.<sup id="cite_ref-Danaher_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Danaher-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On St Brigid's Eve, Brigid was said to visit virtuous households and bless the inhabitants.<sup id="cite_ref-stations_8-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-stations-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> People left items of clothing or strips of cloth outside overnight for Brigid to bless. These were believed to have powers of healing and protection.<sup id="cite_ref-stations_8-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-stations-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Brigid would be symbolically invited into the home and a bed would often be made for her. In some places, a family member who represented Brigid would circle the house three times carrying rushes. They would then knock on the door three times before being welcomed in.<sup id="cite_ref-Danaher_48-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Danaher-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In Ireland and parts of Scotland, a doll representing Brigid would be paraded around the community by girls and young women. Known as the <i><span title="Irish-language text"><i lang="ga">Brídeóg</i></span></i> ('little Brigid'), anglicized 'Breedhoge' or 'Biddy', it was made from rushes or reeds and clad in bits of cloth, flowers, or shells.<sup id="cite_ref-stations_8-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-stations-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> In some areas, a girl took on the role of Brigid. Escorted by other girls, she went house-to-house wearing 'Brigid's crown' and carrying 'Brigid's shield' and 'Brigid's cross', all of which were made from rushes.<sup id="cite_ref-Danaher_48-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Danaher-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Holy_well" title="Holy well">Holy wells</a> are often visited on St Brigid's Day, especially those wells dedicated to her.<sup id="cite_ref-Danaher_48-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Danaher-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>St Brigid's Day parades have been revived in the town of <a href="/wiki/Killorglin" title="Killorglin">Killorglin</a>, County Kerry, which holds a yearly "Biddy's Day Festival". Men and women wearing elaborate straw hats and masks visit public houses carrying a <i>Brídeóg</i> to ward off evil spirits and bring good luck for the coming year.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Relics">Relics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Relics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Reliques_de_Sainte_Brigitte_d%27Irlande.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Reliques_de_Sainte_Brigitte_d%27Irlande.jpg/220px-Reliques_de_Sainte_Brigitte_d%27Irlande.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Reliques_de_Sainte_Brigitte_d%27Irlande.jpg/330px-Reliques_de_Sainte_Brigitte_d%27Irlande.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Reliques_de_Sainte_Brigitte_d%27Irlande.jpg/440px-Reliques_de_Sainte_Brigitte_d%27Irlande.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3096" data-file-height="4128" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Reliques_de_Sainte_Brigitte_d%27Irlande.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A reliquary of Saint Brigid in <a href="/wiki/Old_Saint_Peter%27s_Church,_Strasbourg" title="Old Saint Peter&#39;s Church, Strasbourg">Old Saint Peter's Church, Strasbourg</a></div></div></div>
<p>About the year 878, owing to <a href="/wiki/Viking" class="mw-redirect" title="Viking">Viking</a> raids, Brigid's relics were purportedly taken to <a href="/wiki/Downpatrick" title="Downpatrick">Downpatrick</a> and reburied in the tomb of <a href="/wiki/Saint_Patrick" title="Saint Patrick">St Patrick</a> and <a href="/wiki/Columba" title="Columba">St Columba</a>. The relics of the three saints were said to have been found in 1185 by <a href="/wiki/John_de_Courcy" title="John de Courcy">John de Courcy</a>, and on 9 June of the following year he had them solemnly reburied in <a href="/wiki/Down_Cathedral" title="Down Cathedral">Down Cathedral</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> They are said to have remained in Down Cathedral until 1538, when the relics were desecrated and destroyed during the deputyship of <a href="/wiki/Leonard_Grey,_1st_Viscount_Grane" title="Leonard Grey, 1st Viscount Grane">Lord Grey</a>, excepting Brigid's head which was saved by some of the clergies who took it to the Franciscan monastery of <a href="/wiki/Wiener_Neustadt" title="Wiener Neustadt">Neustadt</a>, in Austria. In 1587 it was presented to the church of the <a href="/wiki/Society_of_Jesus" class="mw-redirect" title="Society of Jesus">Society of Jesus</a> in <a href="/wiki/Lisbon" title="Lisbon">Lisbon</a> by <a href="/wiki/Rudolf_II,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Rudolf II, Holy Roman Emperor">Emperor Rudolph II</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-cm_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cm-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> that is the <a href="/wiki/Igreja_de_S%C3%A3o_Roque" title="Igreja de São Roque">Igreja de São Roque</a> (Church of St Roch), where a frontal part of her skull is still venerated.<sup id="cite_ref-cm_52-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cm-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> However, an occipital part of the skull could already have reached Portugal in the 13th century, preserved in the Igreja São João Batista (Church of St. John the Baptist), on the <a href="/wiki/Lumiar_(Lisbon)" class="mw-redirect" title="Lumiar (Lisbon)">Lumiar</a> (near <a href="/wiki/Lisbon_Airport" title="Lisbon Airport">Lisbon Airport</a>), where it is venerated on 2 February (not 1 February, as in Ireland).<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> According to the local tradition of the latter church, St. Brigid's head would have been carried to <a href="/wiki/Denis_of_Portugal" title="Denis of Portugal">King Dinis of Portugal</a> in 1283 by three Irish knights travelling to the <a href="/wiki/Aragonese_Crusade" title="Aragonese Crusade">Aragonese Crusade</a>. A commemorative inscription on the northern facade of the church, in 16th-century characters, reads: "Here in these three tombs lie the three Irish knights who brought the head of St. Brigid, Virgin, a native of Ireland, whose relic is preserved in this chapel. In memory of which, the officials of the Altar of the same Saint caused this to be done in January AD 1283."<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> It is in fact only from the mid-16th century onwards that this church assumed the invocation of Saint Brígida, when a new side chapel was built and dedicated to her.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1884, <a href="/wiki/Francis_Moran_(cardinal)" title="Francis Moran (cardinal)">Francis Cardinal Moran</a>, <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Bishops_and_Archbishops_of_Sydney" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic Bishops and Archbishops of Sydney">Archbishop of Sydney</a>, obtained a relic of the saint's tooth from
the parochial church of <a href="/wiki/Martin_of_Tours" title="Martin of Tours">St. Martin of Tours</a> in <a href="/wiki/Cologne" title="Cologne">Cologne</a> in the <a href="/wiki/German_Empire" title="German Empire">German Empire</a> and gave it to the Brigidine Sisters in <a href="/wiki/Melbourne" title="Melbourne">Melbourne</a>. <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholic_Church)" title="Cardinal (Catholic Church)">Cardinal</a> Moran wrote about the circumstances in which he obtained the tooth in a letter to the Rev. Mother of this Convent dated 13 March 1906:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>I went all the way to Cologne on my return from Rome in 1884, on my appointment of Archbishop of Sydney to secure a portion of the precious relic of St. Brigid preserved there for over a thousand years. It is venerated at present in the Parochial Church of St. Martin to which in olden times was attached a famous Irish monastery….. The relic is, if I remember aright, a tooth of the Saint. At Cologne, I found great difficulty in securing a portion of this relic. It was at first peremptorily refused. The Pastor of St. Martin's declared that his parishioners would be at once in revolt if they heard that their great parochial treasure was being interfered with. I then had to invoke the aid of an influential Canon of the <a href="/wiki/Cologne_Cathedral" title="Cologne Cathedral">Cathedral of Cologne</a>, whom I had assisted in some of his literary pursuits and he set his heart on procuring the coveted relic. One of his arguments was somewhat amusing: It was the first time that an Irish Archbishop of the remote See of Sydney had solicited a favour from Cologne. It was the new Christian world appealing to the old for a share of its sacred wealth. At all events our pleading was successful and, and I bore away with me a portion of the bone, duly authenticated, which is now the privilege of you good Sisters to guard and venerate….<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>In 1905, Sister Mary Agnes of the <a href="/wiki/Dundalk" title="Dundalk">Dundalk</a> Convent of Mercy took a purported fragment of the skull to St. Bridget's&#32;&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Sic" title="Sic">sic</a></i>&#93; Church in Kilcurry. In 1928, Fathers Timothy Traynor and James McCarroll requested another fragment for St. Brigid's Church in <a href="/wiki/Killester" title="Killester">Killester</a>, a request granted by the <a href="/wiki/Bishop_of_Lisbon" class="mw-redirect" title="Bishop of Lisbon">Bishop of Lisbon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ant%C3%B3nio_Mendes_Belo" title="António Mendes Belo">António Mendes Belo</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The city of <a href="/wiki/Armagh" title="Armagh">Armagh</a> had several associations with St. Brigid. In the twelfth century, the city had two crosses dedicated to Brigid, though, according to the <i>Monasticon Hibernicum</i>, purported relics of the saint reposing in Armagh were lost in an accidental fire in 1179. In the seventeenth century, Armagh also had a street named Brigid located near Brigid's church in the area called "Brigid's Ward."<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Old_Saint_Peter%27s_Church,_Strasbourg" title="Old Saint Peter&#39;s Church, Strasbourg">Old Saint Peter's Church, Strasbourg</a> contains also (unspecified) relics of St. Brigid, brought by the canons of St. Michael in 1398 when they were forced to leave their submerged <a href="/wiki/Honau_Abbey" title="Honau Abbey">abbey of Honau-Rheinau</a>, itself founded by Irish monks.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Iconography">Iconography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Iconography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Cong_St._Mary_of_the_Rosary_Window_St._Brigid_Detail_St._Brigid_holding_her_Lamp_II_2019_09_04.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Cong_St._Mary_of_the_Rosary_Window_St._Brigid_Detail_St._Brigid_holding_her_Lamp_II_2019_09_04.jpg/220px-Cong_St._Mary_of_the_Rosary_Window_St._Brigid_Detail_St._Brigid_holding_her_Lamp_II_2019_09_04.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="319" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Cong_St._Mary_of_the_Rosary_Window_St._Brigid_Detail_St._Brigid_holding_her_Lamp_II_2019_09_04.jpg/330px-Cong_St._Mary_of_the_Rosary_Window_St._Brigid_Detail_St._Brigid_holding_her_Lamp_II_2019_09_04.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Cong_St._Mary_of_the_Rosary_Window_St._Brigid_Detail_St._Brigid_holding_her_Lamp_II_2019_09_04.jpg/440px-Cong_St._Mary_of_the_Rosary_Window_St._Brigid_Detail_St._Brigid_holding_her_Lamp_II_2019_09_04.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2685" data-file-height="3898" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Cong_St._Mary_of_the_Rosary_Window_St._Brigid_Detail_St._Brigid_holding_her_Lamp_II_2019_09_04.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Stained-glass window showing Saint Brigid holding her lamp</div></div></div>
<p>In liturgical iconography and statuary Saint Brigid is often depicted holding a <a href="/w/index.php?title=Cross_of_Saint_Brigid&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Cross of Saint Brigid (page does not exist)">Cross of Saint Brigid</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Crozier" class="mw-redirect" title="Crozier">crozier</a> of the sort used by <a href="/wiki/Abbots" class="mw-redirect" title="Abbots">abbots</a>, and a lamp. Early hagiographers portray Brigid's life and ministry as touched with fire. According to <a href="/wiki/Patrick_Weston_Joyce" title="Patrick Weston Joyce">Patrick Weston Joyce</a>, tradition holds that nuns at her monastery kept an eternal flame burning there.<sup id="cite_ref-Joyce_24-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Joyce-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> She is also often depicted with a cow, or sometimes geese.<sup id="cite_ref-Farmer_3-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Farmer-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Leitmotifs, some of them borrowed from the <a href="/wiki/Apocrypha" title="Apocrypha">apocrypha</a> such as the story where she hangs her cloak on a sunbeam, are associated with the wonder tales of her hagiography and folklore. Cogitosus' circa 650 <i>Vita Sanctae Brigidae</i> portrays Brigid as having the power to multiply such things as butter, bacon, and milk, to bestow sheep and cattle, and to control the weather.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 86">&#58;&#8202;86&#8202;</span></sup>
</p><p>Plant motifs associated with St. Brigid include the white <i><a href="/wiki/Lilium_candidum" title="Lilium candidum">Lilium candidum</a></i> popularly known since medieval times as the Madonna Lily for its association with the <a href="/wiki/Virgin_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="Virgin Mary">Virgin Mary</a>, and the Windflower <i><a href="/wiki/Anemone_coronaria" title="Anemone coronaria">Anemone coronaria</a></i>, called the "Brigid anemone" since the early 19th century.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Kildare, the church of the oak <i><a href="/wiki/Quercus_petraea" title="Quercus petraea">Quercus petraea</a></i>, is associated with a tree sacred to the <a href="/wiki/Druid#Irish_literature_and_law_codes" title="Druid">druids</a>. The colour associated with Brigid is white, worn not only by the Kildare <a href="/wiki/United_Irishmen" class="mw-redirect" title="United Irishmen">United Irishmen</a> during the <a href="/wiki/1798_rebellion" class="mw-redirect" title="1798 rebellion">1798 rebellion</a> but also by <a href="/wiki/Kildare_GAA" title="Kildare GAA">Kildare sports teams</a> in more recent times.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Placenames">Placenames</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Placenames">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Ireland">Ireland</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Ireland">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Kilbride ("Church of Brigid") is one of Ireland's most widely found placenames, there are 43 Kilbrides located in 19 of Ireland's 32 counties:  Antrim (2), Carlow, Cavan, Down, Dublin, Galway, Kildare, Kilkenny (3), Laois, Longford, Louth, Mayo (5), Meath (4), Offaly (4), Roscommon (2), Waterford, Westmeath (2), Wexford (4), and Wicklow (8) as well as two Kilbreedys in Tipperary, Kilbreedia and Toberbreeda in Clare, Toberbreedia in Kilkenny, Brideswell Commons in Dublin, Bridestown and Templebreedy in Cork and Rathbride and Brideschurch in Kildare.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> A number of placenames are derived from <i>Cnoic Bhríde</i> ("Brigid's Hill"), such as <a href="/wiki/Knockbridge" title="Knockbridge">Knockbridge</a> in Louth and <a href="/wiki/Knockbride" title="Knockbride">Knockbride</a> in Cavan.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Wales">Wales</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Wales">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>There are many traditions associating the saint with Wales, with dedications and folklore found across the country. As such, villages are often named for either a church or "<a href="/wiki/Llan_(placename)" title="Llan (placename)">Llan</a>" associated with Bridget. These include the village, castle and parish of <a href="/wiki/St_Brides" title="St Brides">St Brides</a> in <a href="/wiki/Pembrokeshire" title="Pembrokeshire">Pembrokeshire</a> (near <a href="/wiki/St_Brides_Bay" title="St Brides Bay">St Brides Bay</a>), the churches and villages of <a href="/wiki/St._Brides-super-Ely_(Peterston-super-Ely)" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Brides-super-Ely (Peterston-super-Ely)">St. Brides-super-Ely</a> and <a href="/wiki/St_Brides_Major" title="St Brides Major">St Brides Major</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Vale_of_Glamorgan" title="Vale of Glamorgan">Vale of Glamorgan</a>, the church and village of <a href="/wiki/St._Brides_Netherwent" title="St. Brides Netherwent">St. Brides Netherwent</a> in <a href="/wiki/Monmouthshire" title="Monmouthshire">Monmouthshire</a> and the church of <a href="/wiki/St_Brides,_Newport" class="mw-redirect" title="St Brides, Newport">St Brides</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Newport,_Wales" title="Newport, Wales">Newport</a>, the village of <a href="/wiki/Llansanffraid_Glan_Conwy" class="mw-redirect" title="Llansanffraid Glan Conwy">Llansanffraid Glan Conwy</a> in Conwy, <a href="/wiki/Llansantffraid,_Ceredigion" title="Llansantffraid, Ceredigion">Llansantffraid</a> in Ceredigion, and the villages of <a href="/wiki/Llansantffraid-ym-Mechain" title="Llansantffraid-ym-Mechain">Llansantffraid-ym-Mechain</a> and <a href="/wiki/Llansantffraed" title="Llansantffraed">Llansantffraed</a> in <a href="/wiki/Powys" title="Powys">Powys</a>.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Scotland">Scotland</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Scotland">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In Scotland, <a href="/wiki/East_Kilbride" title="East Kilbride">East Kilbride</a> and <a href="/wiki/West_Kilbride" title="West Kilbride">West Kilbride</a> are called after Brigid. <a href="/wiki/Lhanbryde" title="Lhanbryde">Lhanbryde</a>, near <a href="/wiki/Elgin,_Moray" title="Elgin, Moray">Elgin</a>, <a href="/wiki/Scotland" title="Scotland">Scotland</a> is thought to be Pictish for "Church of Brigid". In Toryglen, on Glasgow's southside, there is a Chapel and a Primary School named for St. Brigid; the stained glass windows of the chapel depict St. Brigid's cross.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (January 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="England">England</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: England">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In <a href="/wiki/Fleet_Street" title="Fleet Street">Fleet Street</a>, <a href="/wiki/City_of_London" title="City of London">City of London</a> stands <a href="/wiki/St_Bride%27s_Church" title="St Bride&#39;s Church">St Bride's Church</a>, substantially rebuilt since its foundation in the 600s (7th century).
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Isle_of_Man">Isle of Man</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Isle of Man">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/Isle_of_Man" title="Isle of Man">Isle of Man</a>, where the first name <a href="/w/index.php?title=Breeshey&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Breeshey (page does not exist)">Breeshey</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Manx_language" title="Manx language">Manx</a> form of the name is common, the parish of <a href="/wiki/Bride_(parish)" title="Bride (parish)">Bride</a> is named after the saint.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Elsewhere">Elsewhere</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Elsewhere">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Gross_stmartin_brigida_von_kildare.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Gross_stmartin_brigida_von_kildare.jpg/220px-Gross_stmartin_brigida_von_kildare.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="251" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Gross_stmartin_brigida_von_kildare.jpg/330px-Gross_stmartin_brigida_von_kildare.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Gross_stmartin_brigida_von_kildare.jpg/440px-Gross_stmartin_brigida_von_kildare.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="1140" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Gross_stmartin_brigida_von_kildare.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Brigida von Kildare, Gross St. Martin, Köln</div></div></div>
<ul><li>In the United States, in <a href="/wiki/Marshall_County,_Kansas" title="Marshall County, Kansas">Marshall County, Kansas</a>, is the unincorporated community (or township) St. Bridget, described by some accounts as an extinct town.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/St._Bride%27s,_Newfoundland_and_Labrador" title="St. Bride&#39;s, Newfoundland and Labrador">St. Bride's, Newfoundland and Labrador</a>, at the southwest tip of the <a href="/wiki/Avalon_Peninsula" title="Avalon Peninsula">Avalon Peninsula</a>, is named for St. Brigid, reflecting historical ties to southeastern Ireland</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/St._Brigid_Island" title="St. Brigid Island">St. Brigid Island</a> in <a href="/wiki/Antarctica" title="Antarctica">Antarctica</a> is named after Brigid of Kildare.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other">Other</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Other">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>St. Brigid's popularity made the name Brigid (or its variants such as Brigitte, Bridie, and Bree) popular in Ireland over the centuries. One writer noted that at one time in history "every Irish family had a Patrick and a Brigid".<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Judy_Chicago" title="Judy Chicago">Judy Chicago</a>'s epic feminist artwork <i><a href="/wiki/The_Dinner_Party" title="The Dinner Party">The Dinner Party</a></i> features a place setting for Saint Brigid on the triangular table's second wing, designated for iconographic women from the beginnings of Christianity to the Reformation.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Haitian_Vodou" title="Haitian Vodou">Haitian Vodou</a>, Saint Brigid (along with the goddess Brigid and <a href="/wiki/Mary_Magdalene" title="Mary Magdalene">Mary Magdalene</a>) is <a href="/wiki/Syncretism" title="Syncretism">worshipped as</a> the death loa <a href="/wiki/Maman_Brigitte" title="Maman Brigitte">Maman Brigitte</a>, the consort of <a href="/wiki/Baron_Samedi" title="Baron Samedi">Baron Samedi</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 14">&#58;&#8202;14&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Links_with_Glastonbury">Links with Glastonbury</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Links with Glastonbury">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:St._Brigid_Painting.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/St._Brigid_Painting.jpg/220px-St._Brigid_Painting.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="305" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/St._Brigid_Painting.jpg/330px-St._Brigid_Painting.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/St._Brigid_Painting.jpg/440px-St._Brigid_Painting.jpg 2x" data-file-width="720" data-file-height="999" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:St._Brigid_Painting.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>A painting of Saint Brigid in <a href="/wiki/St._Patrick%27s_Chapel,_Glastonbury" title="St. Patrick&#39;s Chapel, Glastonbury">St. Patrick's Chapel, Glastonbury</a></div></div></div>
<p>St. Brigid has long been linked to <a href="/wiki/Glastonbury" title="Glastonbury">Glastonbury</a>. Sites that depict her include <a href="/wiki/Glastonbury_Tor" title="Glastonbury Tor">Glastonbury Tor</a>, where a stone carving of her milking a cow can be seen above one side of the entrance.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> She also appears in a fresco painting that adorns the interior of <a href="/wiki/St._Patrick%27s_Chapel,_Glastonbury" title="St. Patrick&#39;s Chapel, Glastonbury">St. Patrick's Chapel</a> on the grounds of <a href="/wiki/Glastonbury_Abbey" title="Glastonbury Abbey">Glastonbury Abbey</a> depicting the saint with a spindle, a bowl of fire, and a cow in the background.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It is also documented by <a href="/wiki/William_of_Malmesbury" title="William of Malmesbury">William of Malmesbury</a> that ‘Wherefore the report is extremely prevalent that both Saint Indract and Saint Brigid, no mean inhabitants of Ireland, formerly came over to this spot. Whether Brigid returned home or died at Glastonbury is not sufficiently ascertained, though she left here some of her ornaments; that is to say, her necklace, bag, and implements for embroidering, which are yet shown in memory of her sanctity, and are efficacious in curing divers diseases.’<sup id="cite_ref-ehg_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ehg-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Benedictine Monk <a href="/wiki/John_of_Glastonbury" title="John of Glastonbury">John of Glastonbury</a> wrote in the mid-fourteenth century that the chapel which was excavated in <a href="/wiki/Beckery" title="Beckery">Beckery</a> was named after her; 'Saint Brigid made a stay of several years on an island near Glastonbury, called Bekery or Little Ireland, where there was an oratory consecrated in honour of Saint Mary Magdalene. She left there certain signs of her presence—her wallet, collar, bell, and weaving implements, which are exhibited and honoured there because of her holy memory—and she returned to Ireland, where, not much later, she rested in the Lord and was buried in the city of Down. The chapel on that island is now dedicated in honour of Saint Brigid; on its south side there is an opening through which, according to the belief of the common folk, anyone who passes will receive forgiveness of all his sins.’<sup id="cite_ref-ehg_68-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ehg-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Brides Mound in Beckery is also linked to St. Bridgid and in 2004 'Brigadine sisters, Mary and Rita Minehan, bring the perpetual Brigid flame (restored in 1993) from Solas Bhrde, in Kildare, during a Glastonbury Goddess Conference ceremony on Bride's Mound.'<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="External_links">External links</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brigid_of_Kildare&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: External links">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

</p>

<li id="cite_note-Jestice2004-4"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Jestice2004_4-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"/><cite id="CITEREFJestice2004" class="citation book cs1">Jestice, Phyllis G. (2004). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=H5cQH17-HnMC&amp;pg=PA140"><i>Holy People of the World: A Cross-Cultural Encyclopedia</i></a>. ABC-CLIO. pp.&#160;140–. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/9781576073551" title="Special:BookSources/9781576073551"><bdi>9781576073551</bdi></a><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">1 February</span> 2013</span>. <q>Brigid of Ireland, or of Kildare, has been venerated since the early Middle Ages, along with Patrick and Columba, as one of the three national Christian patron saints of Ireland. By the end of the seventh century, at least two Latin biographies had been written describing her as a nobleman's daughter who chose to consecrate her virginity to God, took the veil as a Christian nun, and became the leader of a community of religious women—or perhaps of both women and men. Certainly, by the 7th century, there was an important double monastery at Kildare that regarded her as its founder.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Holy+People+of+the+World%3A+A+Cross-Cultural+Encyclopedia&amp;rft.pages=140-&amp;rft.pub=ABC-CLIO&amp;rft.date=2004&amp;rft.isbn=9781576073551&amp;rft.aulast=Jestice&amp;rft.aufirst=Phyllis+G.&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DH5cQH17-HnMC%26pg%3DPA140&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3ABrigid+of+Kildare" class="Z3988"></span></span>
